ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-16.ana.ud346e246	I want to start by thanking the Committee for a good and intensive work on this important message at the end of a hectic period here in the house. The Storing Communication is an important part of the follow-up to the December integration agreement last year. At the same time as the notification, a representative proposal from the Storing representatives Lysbakken and Andersen is being considered to ensure integration. It is vital for society to succeed in receiving newly arrived immigrants with a refugee background in such a way that as much as possible can be well integrated, learn the language, get to work quickly and take responsibility for themselves and theirs. Integration work must be long-term, and it requires efforts from all parties: authorities, local communities, volunteering, the labour market and each of those who are going to start their lives in Norway. The faster an asylum seeker gets to live in a municipality, the faster he can participate and contribute his/her resources in working life and society. Therefore, priority must be given to the settlement work in the municipalities. The municipalities now show great ability and willingness to contribute to this work: In 2015 they settled more than 11,300 refugees, an increase of 46 p.m. from the previous year. It is pointed out in the Communication that the Government and the KS will provide an overview of local government expenditure as a host municipality for asylum recipients. It is important that municipalities are compensated for their additional costs in this work. The situation today is that there are almost 4000 refugees in reception waiting for housing, while KS reports vacant dwellings and unused housing decisions in the municipalities. This is not a good resource use, and the Committee therefore asks the government to look at the capacity to conduct the housing interview in reception in order to have a faster settlement. It is also important to give priority to the UDI process so that applications from groups with a high probability of staying can be dealt with quickly. The qualification of the labour market must start as early as possible and be targeted. The Storing Communication suggests that qualification is to a greater extent out-of-work and in cooperation with local business. That's good. It is also important to better facilitate the use of the skills and training provided. It is also a good step in the right direction that the government will grant more asylum seekers temporary work permits by removing the condition of an asylum interview. The Christian People's Party would like the government to go further and do something about the rigid rules of ID clearance. It is difficult to understand that the rules for obtaining work and contributing must be so much more stringent than for the asylum application to be granted. Furthermore, mastering language is essential. It is a key to contacting others, to friendship, cultural understanding and to working in the workplace. Language training must therefore be given priority. The proposal to introduce 50 hours of training in social and cultural knowledge makes sense and can make an important contribution to integration work. However, the majority of the Committee does not want this effort to lead to cutbacks in Norwegian training. Voluntary organisations have a unique role in integration work, and it is important to ensure that receptions involve voluntary actors to a greater extent. Here the proposed scheme of volunteer coordinators receives a good contribution. Children in reception are a vulnerable group who need good care and follow-up. The Christian People's Party is pleased that a Joint Committee is asking the Government to ‘strengthen the child-discipline competence throughout the reception chain’. It has also been agreed, through the examination of the Communication on Integration in the EEA, that an independent system of supervision for beneficiaries of asylum should be established. The capacity of the service must be ensured and the research on integration strengthened. There is also agreement to strengthen the work against radicalisation and violent extremism. There is a formidable integration job around many municipalities. Associations, churches and organizations are making a great effort to establish fatherhood arrangements, create friendships, organise language courses and find foster homes for children on the run. We have to build this under. We must take people's concern, help and sense of responsibility seriously. We have to play these cancers well. Finally, I would like to promote the proposals of the Christian People's Party. The Christian People's Party will vote against proposal No. 9 in the vote, and we propose the following alternative proposals: ‘The government is asking the government to investigate the possibility of withdrawing financial support for religious communities which: 1. encourages the violation of Norwegian criminal law; 2. receives foreign funding from countries that violate fundamental human rights. The government is asked to return to Stortinget in an appropriate manner.»

But it lacks both the heavy and the long-term policies, and that will require this area. We also record that the government has spent a long time – we have requested it for a long time – therefore it was time for the message to arrive. The key to good integration is to learn Norwegian and get into work — and not least stay there. Only in this way can we achieve one of the main objectives of integration policy, namely self-sufficiency. Good language training, education and qualification are essential for success. It must be a primary task to enable local authorities and the labour force to get people into work and participation. We have to make sure that everyone becomes part of the Norwegian social contract: you're going to do your duty, and you're going to demand your right. Here, otherwise, work for every job number one. The labour market has been a success formula that we have benefited from over many years, and the Labour Party believes that strengthening the working line is the answer to today's challenges. The Workers' Party wants asylum seekers to receive training in Norwegian and social subjects. That is why we want to reintroduce 250 hours of Norwegian education. It is strange that the government cuts Norwegian training at reception — what is one of the key factors in success in everyday life and in working life. It is also important to get women to participate in the labour market, and then it is important that we give priority to kindergartens, so that women can get into work. Today, there is a large proportion of immigrant women who do not participate in the labour market. We need to reverse that trend and facilitate it. The answer is not lower pay and temporary. It will only create new class divisions in the workplace. The Labour Party wants to have an active housing policy – increase housing support, provide more rental homes and, above all, strengthen the framework for the Husbank. Finally, perhaps the most important thing: we need to strengthen volunteering. Volunteers are invaluable in the integration work. They need to be recognised and stimulated further — in addition to what we have done today. They're fires that create a better everyday life for everyone. We can do this if we want to, and we in the Workers' Party – we want to. Finally, I take up the Labour Party's proposal.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-16.ana.ud346e404	If you look at last week’s debate on asylum and immigration and at today’s debate so far – and if I imagine how it will turn out – there is a clear link between these issues. There is also no doubt that this is a large matter. This matter really starts far beyond the borders of Norway – in political environments where we have no direct impact at all – and ends in our own communities, in municipal government halls and also in our own neighbourhood. That is what the debate on asylum, immigration and integration policy is about, and that is what it embraces. That is why we must also understand the complexity of the debate. I therefore believe that it was important that the Stortinget came together before Christmas, and that we made an integration settlement. Faced with such a major and complex challenge, it is precisely the sum of the votes of the Norwegian National Assembly that will create policies and solutions that will give legitimacy to the population in the coming years. With that in mind, I think it was a pity that SV chose to stand outside, but I would nevertheless like to thank SV for a good cooperation in the committee in dealing with this matter. In fact, if we fail to integrate, we are also at risk of increased social disparities. The challenge we're facing now is of a completely different dimension and size than ever before. One cannot compare this with the 1970s, when a work immigrant came on Saturday and was working on Monday – or with the labour immigration we have seen in the last 10–15 years from many Eastern European countries, where people like to have a job before coming to Norway. Here's a completely different target group we're going to work with. It is then essential that we have an integration policy where the municipalities are also involved, and that we ensure that the way from being a refugee to becoming an integrated and inclusive citizen is as short as possible. The integration debate in the future must be about political realities such as housing, work, welfare services, language and not least municipal economy – where this is seen in the context, and I refer to the debate we had on the municipal proposition yesterday. Therefore, it is also important that the government has provided an integration message that goes deep and addresses the challenges and not least the opportunities. The last time this kind of new crime was done in the integration field was when Erna Solberg was the municipal minister. I know I'm being criticized by the Workers' Party to talk about history, but a historical party of the Workers' Party's calibres has to bear in mind that the rest of us also refer to history sometimes. There has been no new work on this field since then. This is the largest single document in many years, given the width of the case complex and how many proposals are on the table. I don't want to spend my allocated time on any reading from the message, so let me draw up some areas that I think are important, and that I want us to have in mind in the dialogue and the debate ahead. First of all, you're working on how to start integration a lot earlier. It is not uncommon for an asylum seeker to sit on reception for too long and to be passive before he or she has a municipality to live in, but this is not a new situation – something that has been going on for many years. This happens very often without initiating language training or other types of action that should be there and that are now coming through this message. We are now also making it easier to get a temporary work permit. We've got a lot of suggestions on how to do this. The working party has submitted comments on this. But this is the first time we see a proposal where – given that the identity is clarified, because it is crucial – we open up to see how to make it easier to get temporary work permits. We strengthen the role of volunteering so that they can contribute to Norwegian training and other integration measures. We introduce skills mapping and subsequent career guidance. And it is precisely at that stage – when you have arrived at a reception and are about to start a life in Norway – that you also need to be trained in how our society is connected. Social knowledge – our values, our culture and our laws – is important knowledge in the first phase of a life in a reception. It is also crucial for the further journey. We have seen countless examples from different municipalities that say that if they settle for refugees who have received some form of social education — what it is like to live in the Norwegian community, how to apply for a job, how to get to work, and how to get your own housing — the integration into the municipality becomes quite different. The fact that we use social skills to have a common platform from the start is crucial. Then it's the housing model: I'm very happy that the government determines that we're going to have voluntary housing. This is because Norwegian municipalities have never previously settled as many people as they do now. This year, municipalities have decided to settle over 16 000 refugees. That's a completely different result from what we had when we took over after the red-green ones, and it was a aftermath that was there even after a period of time without the enormous pressure that we've had. And this has been done without coercion, without overrun, but through dialogue and not least by creating a welcome culture in the community. It is not only important to provide enough housing when talking about housing, we also need to adapt the whole apparatus needed to facilitate good integration. UDI already has good results from municipalities that have tried out systematic self-settlement within the municipality in question where refugees have been accommodated. This means that it is not self-dwelling of the model where you can only choose where you want to live, but according to the agreed quota the municipality has agreed to, and in consultation with the municipality concerned. Furthermore, it is important to remember that this government has increased the grant to rental dwellings. The government introduced an additional grant for the housing of lone minors and has already notified it to continue in 2017. The next point is the introduction scheme. It has also remained unchanged for too many years, and it is within which a refugee actually spends the first two years of his life as a new one in a municipality. On my journey around municipalities in Norway, I have seen that the quality is too varied. There's too little focus on getting into work quickly. You don't differentiate enough. It's equal to education for everyone, and it also makes that there aren't enough people coming out of work or education. It is therefore important to see language training, working practices, education and local community involvement in the context of this programme. Municipalities that view integration as a task similar to school, care and other, deliver good results. But if this just rolls and goes on the side, you won't get it. That is precisely why I believe that one of the most important things that is happening in this message is this review of the introduction system. I think that if, after the first two years, we get more out of work or education, so that they can start a career, we'll get completely different results in the long-term integration work than we've had so far. Finally, when all fine speeches are made, political decisions are made and the systems are in place, this is also about how we accept our new countrymen. Have you got an immigrant in the neighborhood, or a new neighbor? Have the children had an immigrant boy in class, or a new classmate? Have you got a new immigrant woman at work, or a new colleague? We have to make sure to create a welcome culture in our communities, for this job we're going to do together. We've done it before, and we're going to do it again. I also think that the Communication of Integration responds well, in particular by taking into account the active role of volunteering. One question I can ask myself is: is it so that my generation with a minority background was the last one to have a good chance of success in Norway? The answer to that is of course no, because I think the best stories are still ahead of us. But then we have to dry up the debates we have now, and we have to promote new policies, as we are doing now.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-16.ana.ud346e528	The most important thing for us in the Progress Party in this matter has been to get the other parties to recognize that there is a connection between the number of immigrants coming, and the possibility of integration. That's something we've been dealing with all alone, but now the bulk of the population is of the same opinion, and we're very happy about that. The reality is that after decades of massive immigration into Europe and Norway, the experience, based on all available public empirical evidence, statistics and knowledge, is that integration is far more difficult to achieve than immigration liberal parties and NGOs have claimed. Yes, integration acts almost like an illusion when we see the reality in our eyes and what are the results after decades of immigration: ‘Dropout’ among non-Western immigrant boys is three times higher than that of other students. Labour participation among the largest non-western immigrant groups is minimal, even after a long period of residence and they have acquired Norwegian citizenship and are almost non-existent for immigrant women from some countries. The same groups are significantly and significantly overrepresented in the receipt of economic social assistance and other welfare schemes. Six out of ten children living in child poverty have a migrant background. Immigrants are clearly overrepresented in criminal statistics, for a number of serious offences. The main reason for increased social disparities is immigration, and unfortunately much indicates that these changes will be quite permanent, and permanent sub-classes will arise as a result of immigration and the fact that social differences and challenges are inherited. These are just some of the areas where the challenges are well documented. Then there are areas where there is probably not as much evidence, professional empirical evidence and statistics, but where there are clear challenges. There are a number of challenges to the repression and social control of immigrants carried out by other immigrants. Here too, unfortunately, there is a wide range of examples of everything from moral police to the hot of immigrant children, and especially girls who do not appear as religious enough, pressure to wear hijab – yes, even ethnic Norwegian children who are offered to use hijab in order not to be bullied and stand out at the school leisure system in the inner east of Oslo. It is very serious that people who have fled from regimes where that type of assault is being carried out must have experienced that what they have escaped from has followed them to where they have applied for protection. On top of this again, challenges arise with radicalisation and the spread of extreme groupings and attitudes in Norway and other European countries, which we have unfortunately seen in recent times. Despite the fact that these developments have not occurred overnight, but have evolved over many decades, those who have faced a liberal policy of immigration, and the NGOs, have tried as well as they can not just reveal the truth: they have tried to establish truths, and they have created an environment where those who have pointed out challenges point to the problems of society, have been hottered. They've been downgraded, they've been humiliated. It has had a major and serious impact on their career opportunities in society, and it has led to major social challenges. What's interesting is to look at the experiment NRK P3 started in that context, where a reporter who posted articles from well-known British newspapers with statistics on what's about extremism among immigrants, was almost excluded from the workplace – people wouldn't eat lunch with him, etc. These are the results of the environment created for those who want to point out the facts in these cases. At the same time, one has also made a vigorous effort to create parallel truths, which fortunately no longer goes. The best example of this was when the fractional leader of the Workers' Party, Helga Pedersen, was arrested by Nettavisen when she presented one of these truths, namely that integration is going so well, mainly integration is going very well, and it has done so. What's new is you start to confront that kind of statement and say, "Excuse me, what statistics, what empire, what area of society are you referring to where you think integration has been enriching and going very well?" Because what we have of the available data in this field shows something else. Much of this is due to the fact that, as massive immigration to Europe and Norway has taken place, it has also developed its own immigration and integration industries that convert billions of dollars. NOAS is the Norwegian Association for Asylum Seekers, but the Anti-racist Centres, Red Cross, Redd Barna, MiRA Centre, SEIF, OMOD, the Association of Advocates and a number of other organisations have also evolved to become the ‘Norwegian Organisation for Asylum Seekers’, or more specifically the ‘Norwegian Organisation for Increasing Immigration and Asylum Seekers’. None of these organisations are ever held responsible for their policies and the consequences of their policies. They may establish truths such as that family reunification is the key to integration. No one in society challenges those who make that kind of claims. I'm sorry, but here it is that the largest non-Western immigrant group in Norway is Somali, with about a quarter of them. 36 000 people. The main reason for this increase has been family reunification. This is a group that unfortunately comes out poorly and poorly in a number of areas of Norwegian society. How has a liberal family reunification policy helped this group to be well integrated into Norwegian society? No one is questioning. Or: How is it that Sweden, which has had one of the most liberal policies on immigration, family reunification and all the points relating to immigration, is now in a disaster situation for the nation, where they are struggling physically to deal with what has arisen as a result of immigration? It is important to point out evolution and what are the realities, to talk about more of the same things that have not helped us, does not lead us further. That is why, in fact, the most important integration measure is to achieve a restrictive Norwegian immigration and asylum policy. The results of decades of immigration to Norway show that this is not the case; this is no better as people live in Norway. The problems are escalating, and eventually they become as big as we can see in our neighbouring country of Sweden, where there are simply areas that are unsafe for those who live there, but also for auxiliaries and important institutions like the police, who want to enter these areas. It is therefore strange to hear that a party like the Workers' Party, which has had the main responsibility in this field for very many years, has suddenly become involved in immigration and only talks about some symptoms and more money to the same thing that has not worked, instead of looking at what has gone fundamentally wrong in the policy they have been involved in. The main party responsible for immigration to Norway is also the party that criticises the Progress Party for not having done enough for integration. If the policy of the Progress Party had been enforced in this field from the 1980s until today, the challenges would not be what we see today. Integration would also have a completely different form, because we are also willing to do something about welfare systems and incentives to be integrated. When there is no roof over welfare schemes, and a single non-Western immigrant mother can get benefits equivalent to NOK 700,000-800 000 in wages, it is not strange that people do not get into work. When we just look at what housing alone represents of the cost of living for some in the largest cities, it's not strange you don't get to integrate. These are points that would really do something about integration if you were serious about it. Unfortunately, however, such proposals have always been put to the vote, and the Workers' Party has – after pursuing irresponsible policies in this field as well – suddenly suspended itself in the cash support, which I hear they are repeating today. Cash support is the smallest of the contributions received by non-Western immigrants. There are far larger welfare systems on which non-Western immigrants are overrepresented. We have never heard the Workers' Party be willing to do anything with those arrangements. Therefore, I would like to stress that for the Progress Party, the most important integration policy is to do something about the number that comes, because we see a relationship between the number of people that come and what we have the real ability to integrate.

Language is essential for integration into society, and we are therefore disappointed that the government wants to cut Norwegian education. We are positive that a desire for more education in cultural and social knowledge in the receptions, but since that training is intended to take place in the mother tongue, we do not believe that there is a basis for cutting Norwegian education. The result is that people who wait several years for reception may lose valuable professional skills and motivation. There is an obvious potential for efficiency improvement here by distinguishing groups with a high probability of residence, as the government already does with asylum seekers with a high probability of refusal. People in asylum recipients can make an effort; they have both the competence and the desire to contribute to society around them. Both the state framework, the municipalities and the volunteering have to put up the work in such a way that they have an opportunity. The left has special involvement with children in general and with refugee children in particular. We are therefore pleased that the government gives more children the opportunity to go to kindergarten. We should like to see one go even further and equalize kindergarten children and schoolchildren. Kindergarten is the very best integration venue for children with refugee backgrounds, and it also promotes the integration of parents and provides the whole family with a valuable Norwegian network. Children who come here alone are a particularly vulnerable group. However, as the Communication points out, these young people are rapidly integrated into society. Three out of four between 18 and 29 are in work or education or in an introduction programme. Interestingly, the proportion of young people living in Afghanistan is highest in 2009–2010. After four years in Norway, they are at the level of peers in the population. In other words, the integration of children who come alone has been a success. That is why I am pleased that the proposal for temporary residence for all children who come alone was voted down during the treatment of Prop. This Regulation shall enter into force on the twentieth Integratingly, the proposal would be a disaster. It is border controls in countries near us and far away from us that ensure that fewer people come to Norway, it is not fear-terrorism on the part of the government that takes care of it. Integrating vulnerable people who come to a new country requires first and foremost that all of us who are part of Norwegian society, including them, give trust and welcome, as the representative Kapur pointed out a little while ago. The most important thing is the personal meetings in the store, in the café, in the street, at home and at work. If we meet people with fear and prejudice because they are asylum seekers or immigrants, we will not succeed in integrating them. Nor will we succeed in making demands and getting those demands met, and we will not succeed in having expectations and thinking that they will be fulfilled. Then is the last thing we need, a state council that legitimizes fear, prejudice and exclusion by linking immigrants to concern for the future of children. That kind of rhetoric creates a distinction between us and them, which you shouldn't look far away or far back, in geography or in history, to see how dangerous it is, how toxic it is for the possibilities of including new people in Norwegian society. The fact that people get into activity and get into paid work quickly is absolutely crucial. It's devastating to sit and stare in the wall at a reception for 12 or 14 months, and that's why we support it. removing the requirement for an asylum interview before granting a temporary work permit. Language, of course, is crucial. Norwegian training needs to be strengthened. We have to get back to at least 250 hours of Norwegian education, and we don't have to cut the number of hours, as originally suggested. 50 hours of cultural and social knowledge is a good suggestion, but it must be in addition to and not in place of language training. Then we will vote against IX in the vote, on the investigation of an opportunity to draw support to religious organizations that express things that we don't like in Norwegian society. We, as a party, are at least as concerned with combating intolerance as everyone else, but we are also a champion of freedom of expression, and that it should extend far. We are therefore pleased that the Christian People's Party has promoted an alternative and somewhat milder proposal, and we will consider voting subsidiaryly for that proposal.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-16.ana.ud346e830	The basic plan of the government's integration policy is for immigrants to live and live in Norway to come to work, learn Norwegian, accept Norwegian values and become active citizens of society. The alternative to successful integration is non-life and often as passive recipients of public benefits. It affects the individual and challenges the sustainability of the Norwegian welfare society. The welfare model developed in Norway through generations demands that we manage to create taxpayers of those who will live in Norway and prevent too many from living on public schemes. Norwegian society is in the middle of a big task. Never before have so many migrants come to Norway as last year, and never before have we had to integrate as many people who have stayed in Norway as they do now. In addition, many who have come to Norway in the past and have been granted refugee status are outside the labour market and are struggling to be integrated into Norwegian society many years after they have arrived here. Large groups of immigrants are out of work. Among African immigrant women, there are only 37.5 pst. linked to the labour market, towards 66 pst. among Norwegian women. These numbers are gloomy and show the whites what a big challenge we're facing. I would like to stress that integration is not quick-fix, which reminds me of every single time I visit municipalities and I am presented for the job done. Nor is it a task that we can succeed in without the individual being integrated and willing to put down the self-help that is necessary. No politician or anyone else can decide that someone is integrated. We can make arrangements, but the job has to be done to a large extent by the individual himself. The number to be integrated will be very important for how well we can succeed. That is why the tightening up adopted by the Stortingen last week is also an important premise for the success of integration policy. Many of those who come lack education. Some are illiterate and some lack work experience. Although you have both education and work experience, the leap to the Norwegian labour market can be formidable for many. The Norwegian labour market is characterised by a small number of unskilled jobs, and training is required to perform very many jobs. The pressure on the part of the labour market where you can enter without formal education will be high in the coming years. Success in integration is also about getting a good interaction between the individual, the state, the municipality, the business community and, in particular, volunteering. Among other things, I have have visited several communities that are making considerable efforts to accommodate people with a refugee background in Norway. Integration policy is important in order to avoid parallel societies characterised by lawlessness and open crime. We see that this is something that characterizes more countries in Europe. We've been spared this in Norway, but we'll never take it for granted. The government includes this Communication on new approaches to integration policy, a Communication that launches 69 solutions to achieve integration outcomes. Refugees and migrants coming to Norway are to be trained in Norwegian culture and social skills from one day to the next. Norwegian laws, regulations and values are something that everyone in Norway has to accept and deal with. Nobody puts their positions back on the frontier. That's why it's important. Integration receptions for those with a high probability of stay and motivation shall have a full-time programme and shall be able to initiate the introduction programme already in receipt. The government already wants to set up such receptions with about 500 seats this year. If this is a success, we'll create more of these receptions. The most important thing is the result. The government believes that instinctive and motivation are fundamental to successful integration. Therefore, we will reward those who are on and show results through the process of integration. If the result of self-help fails, we also believe that the reward should be missed. Too few immigrants are active in professional life, and only 15 pst. are in working practice within two years with an introductory programme. It's just to imagine being put in a classroom day in and day out for two years to learn language. Activity and practice are the key to learning languages and Norwegian working life. To do this, we want to introduce a requirement that practice be assessed on a case-by-case basis and combined with Norwegian training. We must bear in mind that the alternative to successful integration is passiveness and dependence on public benefits. Everyone living in Norway should have equal opportunities, but with opportunities and freedom it is equally natural to make demands. Therefore, we will require five years of residence to receive cash support, that immigrant women in maternity leave also participate in the training, and we will reward work and performance over inaction. I am pleased that there is a broad consensus on much in integration policy. As responsible politicians, we need to think for the long term on behalf of the country and take action to preserve the Norwegian welfare model for future generations.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-16.ana.ud346e1625	This is a very important matter that concerns how we can take care of fellow people who are on the run, and on the weakest of them. We in the Progress Party believe that it is necessary to look at some structural changes in order to improve the assistance and protection of women and children in refugees. If we look at the numbers and realities in this field, it turns out that in 2015 was 77 pst. by asylum seekers to Norway men. This is a rule that we also see in other countries, namely that women remain in the neighbourhoods, while men seek asylum in Europe. Thus, the Asylum Institute helps mainly men, while emergency aid in the neighbourhoods primarily helps women and children in need. The most targeted measures to assist women in refugees must therefore be excluded from the asylum system. Both neighbourhood assistance and the UN quota refugee scheme are much better aid schemes for women and children than the asylum institution. Today’s asylum institute requires asylum seekers to put their feet on Norwegian or European soils to seek asylum. This means that asylum seekers must undertake a long and dangerous journey in order to seek protection through the asylum system. According to the UN, women and children are very vulnerable during these journeys. Unfortunately, abuse and violence are relatively widespread, especially against women travelling alone. This is one of the main reasons why the Progress Party has long said that reform of the asylum system is desirable in order to use the UN quota refugee regime. In this way, those who do not have physical, mental and financial resources to travel half the world to seek protection, seek help and seek protection in nearby camps. Secondly, people can be selected and taken out according to the capacity of the different countries. Women will thus avoid embarking on a life-threatening journey to Europe and huge resources will be released to assist the neighbourhoods and to the UN quota refugee scheme. Unlike the asylum system, quota refugees are representative of women and children. The right-hand Party Government has given priority to picking up entire families as quota refugees and has its own quota for refugees with major medical needs. There are, as we know, 60 million people on the run in the world today. About 98 pst. of refugees with a need for assistance are located in the neighbourhoods and do not seek asylum in Europe or elsewhere. Among those who actually seek asylum, there is a great overrepresentation of young men. Sweden alone spends more resources on 160 000 asylum seekers than the UN spends on 60 million refugees worldwide. The migration agency's estimate for direct asylum costs is 61 billion. Swedish krona for 2016, while UN High Commissioner for Refugees in 2016 has a budget of 56.3 billion. kr. It goes without saying that with such a biased priority as to where you spend the large amount of money versus where the need for help is greater, we can't reach it. That's why we in the Progress Party are happy and proud that we in the government have been involved in granting 10 billion. DKK over a four-year period for Syrian refugees, which is a record contribution. At the same time, the government has also run a donor country conference, where it has collected approx. 12 B.C.E. dollars for the world's refugees. That way, we can help more people who are in need and who need protection. The situation of women in refugees is one of the most important arguments for a complete reform of the asylum institution, and I hope that more and more, both representatives and actors in this field, realise and help us get to it as soon as possible.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-16.ana.ud346e1646	The representative proposal from the Left addresses a very important topic. The centre party believes that lone women and young girls living in reception must receive adequate protection and follow-up. The Centre Party will therefore support the proposals promoted by the minority of the Committee. It concerns the proposal to increase the number of staff at reception, the proposal to carry out an assessment of all reception centres to identify the need for conversion in order to ensure women's access to safe detention centres, the proposal to ensure long-term training on gender-based violence for staff at reception, and the proposal to facilitate the necessary cooperation between reception centres, local health authorities, women's organisations, crisis centres and other relevant institutions at local and regional level. The centre party notes that there is a need to improve the quality of reception and to strengthen the skills of staff. In this respect, I would like to mention that, in its attitude to the Communication of Integration, the Committee asks the Government to establish independent supervisory arrangements for asylum reception operations. Here I would like to make it clear that the Central Party believes that this type of supervision can well be the responsibility of the State. We do not want further red tape and the creation of our own supervisory bodies. That being said, we support independent supervision. The aim must be to have reception that gives the residents a good offer, so that they can feel safe and safe.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-16.ana.ud346e1661	When conflicts or natural disasters strike, it affects men, boys, women and girls in different ways. ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-16.ana.ud346e2135	It was a great way out of the building that you were standing behind the Civil Ombudsman. I can join that part of the statement that expresses a general goodwill towards a valuable institution. But that doesn't mean that the laws in which the Stortinget has authorized the Civil Ombudsman, and the assumptions that have led the whole institute, no longer apply. I'll elaborate on it a little bit more. It is true that no matter what we as individuals may feel about the individual cases presented in this particular communication, it is a wild legal reality that the Stortinget did not intend to confer a binding decision-making power on the Civil Ombudsman. This means that the Civil Ombudsman may not review or annul an otherwise lawful decision. He has to live with that, even though he doesn't like all the rashes this brings. The reason for the introduction of the ombudsman system in the area of administration was that it should be a complement to ordinary judicial review. It was to be a cheap, simple and relatively quick offer to individuals who considered themselves unduly affected by administrative decisions. The simplified procedure of the Civil Ombudsman is not always sufficient to assess the case on the basis, for example: relevant counter-respects, such as full counter-rejection in ordinary judicial proceedings may offer. The role of the Civil Ombudsman demonstrates its special justification where unfairness towards a specific party is pointed out – often in the face of an excessive bureaucracy – and where such an assessment of an external and respected professional body leads to the relevant management team being aware of the relevance and importance of the arguments put forward and, as a result, to act following the opinion of the Civil Ombudsman. That's the authority that the Storting has assumed. It is true in our time that there is no one who can immediately claim to have the employment authority. Those times are over. The authority you need must be built up by professional depth and knowledge. That is the kind of authority to which it is intended, at least when in this context we have referred to that authority. It would be contradictory to say that it weakens the Civil Ombudsman because he is being contradicted. What kind of authority is that? It must be a very poorly professionally justified authority that does not tolerate some resistance and counter-arguments. It's not the employment authority we're talking about here, it's the kind of professional knowledge that matters get through the discussion with the Civil Ombudsman. I do not ignore the fact that there are many not entirely optimal administrative decisions that just make such a critical review by the Civil Ombudsman necessary and valuable. In its communication under paragraph 2, the Civil Ombudsman has himself stated: ‘The Ombudsman’s opinions are not legally binding, nor can the Ombudsman reverse decisions taken by the Ombudsman. However, the size has provided that the administration complies with the Ombudsman’s opinions.’;» I very much agree with these assessments. I'm also glad to hear that in practice it happens in most cases. Perhaps the Storting should be more briefed on the statistics on how many issues are not actually followed up. It could have been interesting management information. The civil officer continues and says: ‘The Civil Ombudsman is appointed by the Stortinget to supervise the administration. It is therefore particularly unfortunate if the administration does not follow the Ombudsman’s opinions on the interpretation of laws adopted by the Stortinget. This undermines the Ombudsman’s ability to carry out his duties and weakens parliamentary control of the administration.’.» Then I think maybe the next proofreading should look through these formulations one more time. The majority actually uses parliamentaryism to control the administration. It is the government that is at the top of the administration, and it is the government that may have to address its requirements and control. Otherwise we would get it in the back door. In particular, if the Storting says that individual matters which we in this way indirectly decide on should be closely linked to parliamentary control. We would then have something we should not want in our constitution, namely a parliamentary control body and thus a short way to the rulings of party politics in the resolution of specific individual cases. The greatest and foremost task of the size is to design general legal standards, and then to entrust them to the judiciary and to the administration on the basis of established criteria. Jussen can sometimes produce results that may seem unreasonable to an individual by its system logic. The civilian ombudsman's strength lies in entering and viewing such conclusions from the small man's perspective and, if necessary, recommending another solution. If the legal rules prevent this, it is necessary to draw attention to weaknesses in the law, which the Storting can do as a legislator. Further, I would like to point out that in cases there is access to contribute to free handling, where the case can be fully resolved in the court. I believe that this should be a particularly topical procedure in which the interests of two individuals compete and where the need for a proper legal solution is great. I would like to stress why I believe that an independent court hearing is preferable as a solution in such cases. This is because the courts are handling the cases that are being referred to them, but without initiating them. It may be said that the Civil Ombudsman does not initiate the cases that are being referred to, but he has such a vast slicing authority that he can in practice choose the matters he thinks are particularly interesting to pursue. Then you can get into the situation that it is the Civil Ombudsman who, through his selection, chooses the side of one private party against the other and then acts as a representative for the other. It doesn't give much legitimacy to that of the private parties that are losing, and that, in fact, according to our rules of play, have to come to terms with the result. Without going into the details of the matter, it has been difficult in the special communication to see the small man who has suffered under an unfair administrative decision. On the contrary, I have seen it there that the small man has been granted a building permit by the administration, and that perhaps the case handler of the Civil Ombudsman if he had been employed in a municipality, would have refused him. If I were to provide the Civil Ombudsman with advice – which he is, of course, quite free to follow – it would have to be necessary to use the special means of communicating in particular to clarify the core area of the Civil Ombudsman's task, namely, where the system logic of the administration has contributed to concretely unfair results for individuals. Here it seems – if I can allow myself the tip formulation – that it is the system logic and the ideal interests that have been realised in relation to the concrete basic ownership interests, what is experienced at the level of the citizen. Otherwise, I will round off these considerations by referring to our state custom based on the principle of power distribution. It might as well be called the max division principle, so that no instance is superfluous. This means that it is the Stortinget that is to decide on the general legal standards to be applied. Concrete application of law is added to the administration authorised for this purpose or to the courts. It is this idea that is behind Storting's choice that the civil ombudsman's opinions have never been considered legally binding. As a result, there may arise, and I would like to add that there should be, situations in which a competent administrative body which has taken a valid decision maintains its understanding of the law, even if the Civil Ombudsman has another. Finally, I got some challenges from the committee leader. I believe I have replied to this with authority that the authority is not the employment authority, but the professional depth of the Civil Ombudsman can bring a case. Having regard to the considerations that the Stortinge did when the law on the Ombudsman of the Stortinge for the administration was passed, I would argue that if the Stortinge had at the time seen that there should be a one-to-one relationship between the Ombudsman's opinions and the effectiveness of the administration, we would not have had any institution of the Ombudsman. Then we'd have an administrative court. Stortinget did not explicitly go into that. I would also like to make it clear that it is entirely unproblematic for us to support that proposal, because we see that the administration largely follows the advice given to them by the Civil Ombudsman. Furthermore, such a decision will not alter the competence or duties of the Civil Service Ombudsman. Otherwise, the pre-workers who followed the drafting of the Civil Ombudsman Act will continue to act as law-makers, no matter how we choose to design the words of a decision today.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-16.ana.ud346e2214	The Civil Ombudsman is the chief executive officer of the Stortinge. He is an important contributor to the distribution of power between administration, Storting and the court. One of the most important tasks of the Civil Ombudsman is to prevent the administration from doing wrong. Under his own law, he will report to Stortinget both annually and when it is otherwise appropriate. So he is one of the tools that Stortinget has equipped to ensure that the government complies with the laws that Stortinget has given. That's why it's unfortunate when the administration of two issues that we're discussing today, chooses to ignore the Civil Ombudsman's statements. Of course, the Ombudsman’s opinions are not legally binding, there is no disagreement, nor can he reverse decisions taken by the administration. However, Civil Ombudsman Falkanger has made it clear to the Committee that the Stortinget has provided that the administration complies with the opinion of the Civil Ombudsman. When the administration chooses not to do so, one of the main functions of the Civil Ombudsman is also to be able to report this to Stortinget, and that is what the Civil Ombudsman did in this case when he delivered a separate notice to Stortinget. The fact that the administration chooses to disregard the view of the Civil Ombudsman in these specific communications may help to undermine the Ombudsman’s ability to carry out his mission and thus, in principle, weaken parliamentary control over the administration. The two issues that we are talking about here, the two specific messages, are different in nature. However, in each way they have such a fundamental importance for the ombudsman system that it is good that the Storting became aware of them, and the Greens are pleased that the Civil Ombudsman used his access to alert them to the Stortinget. None of these matters are in themselves the matter of the control committee, but they show very clearly the primary importance of the Civil Ombudsman's follow-up to the cases. Thus, the most fundamental issue is that of building bans in the beach zone and whether it has an effect on older control plans, and here the Civil Ombudsman is quite clear in his interpretation that the building ban in the beach zone will also apply to older control plans, and says that «the beach zone will be expanded contrary to the intentions of the legislator». Fredrik Holth and Nikolai Winge from Norway's environmental and life sciences universities received two years ago an assignment from the Ministry of the Environment to develop a new law counsellor in the beach zone. The current Ministry of the Environment largely agreed with these interpretations, but disagreed on one point, namely that the building ban in the coastal zone should not have an effect on older regulatory plans that allow for development. This guide has not yet been published. Of course, it is not up to the Greens to decide whether this is due to the legal disagreement, but it is at least unfortunate that this remains an undecided point. The Ministry, in its proposal to amend the Law on Planning and Building, which was sent to the hearing in August, proposed that their interpretation be clarified in the legislative text. So the Ministry's understanding of law is based on taking these possible legislative changes, which are the ministry's view, in advance. That is why the Civil Ombudsman’s opinion is very principleal and can have considerable practical consequences, because it means that all municipalities with older control plans in the beach zone must develop new plans or change the plans, so that they do not allow them to permit development. It is quite understandable that this is a difficult political problem, but it is therefore very important to decide how this matter should be interpreted and what is the intention of the legislator, and it is very important that we take into account what the Civil Ombudsman says on such a question, because this case shows the consistency of choosing one or the other interpretation. In the opinion of the Green Party, this is a very good example of the department having to take the Civil Ombudsman's view into account and translate it into practice by sending out the message about the correct interpretation of the law to the municipalities. If this does not happen, if we continue to have a situation where the department ignores the interpretation of the Civil Ombudsman's law, we will also have a situation where it is very unclear to what extent it is necessary for the departments to comply with the Ombudsman's views on similar fundamental issues.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-16.ana.ud346e2229	In the configuration of the Control and Constitutional Committee, the following recommendations are made: ‘The Government of the Central Government is asked to accept and submit to the opinion of the Civil Ombudsman in general’. I agree with this recommendation. The Ombudsman of the Grand Duchy of Luxembourg has a very important role to play in controlling the decisions of the administration. Although the opinion of the Civil Ombudsman is not formally binding, such opinions must, as a general rule and in general, be followed up by the administration for the proper functioning of the scheme. Therefore, it is only in very special cases that the administration does not follow the Ombudsman. An overview from the Civil Ombudsman sent to the Swedish Research Office in January this year shows that this has happened in 25 cases from 2004 until today. 20 of these cases were dealt with by the previous government. In a recent coincidence, this has occurred in two cases under the Ministry of Municipal Affairs and Modernisation. In these two cases, following a thorough professional assessment, the ministry found it appropriate to maintain its previous position after the Ombudsman’s opinion. At the same time, there was a need to clarify the changes to the Law on Planning and Building, which is followed up. As mentioned, I believe that the Ombudsman has a very important role to play in controlling the administration. The Ombudsman shall, in accordance with the law of the Ombudsman, § 3 ‘seek to ensure that there is no injustice to the individual citizen in the public administration’. It is important that the individual citizen be able to complain to the Civil Ombudsman and not go to the courts. The two specific issues raised in the communication relate to minor measures where authorisation has been granted under the Planning and Building Act. There are neighbors who have complained to the Ombudsman. These are not individuals who have not been authorised, but really are issues where there is disagreement between neighbours. The most important and most fundamental of the two cases relate to the construction ban in the beach zone and to the relationship to older control plans without a specific building limit. This is a general question of the interpretation of the Law on Planning and Building, and this is the issue of interpretation dealt with by the Ministry. The Ministry’s interpretation is based on the Act’s transitional provision and has been based on the Ministry ever since the Act on Planning and Building of 27 June 2008 entered into force on 1 July 2009. This means that this interpretation of the law has been based on the previous government and on the current government. The rapporteur's observations asked whether the practice was a political desire for liberalisation. I can refute that. Here we have followed the same practice as the previous government. The position of the Ministry results in the requirement for a specified building limit to be applied only to newer plans. It implies that plans adopted before 1 July 2009 continue to apply even if they do not have the construction limit. The tightening of the building ban in the 100-metre belt along the sea, which took place in the 2008 Planning and Building Act, was intended to work only in the future. It was not meant to set aside previously adopted plans. If this were meant to be the case, it would have economic and administrative consequences so much that it would be discussed in detail in the billing proposal. Let me also refer to the Ministry of the Environment's guidance on statesmen, municipalities and others. The Department of the Environment’s counsellor T-1491 Area Area of the Municipality Plan, published in 2012, reads as follows on page 92: ‘With reference to the transitional provision of the fourth paragraph of Section 34-2, the provision is understood as meaning that the construction ban does not apply where the whole or part of the prohibitive zone is outsourced to construction areas on a plan adopted under the 1985 Planning and Building Act.’ This shows that the departments have been consistent in their guidance on how to interpret this law. The former Ministry of the Environment and the position of the Ministry of Municipal Affairs and Modernisation has been used for a long time and, in my view, provides a sensible solution in practice. The Ministry has therefore proposed that this be clarified in the law. It would create uncertainty if the interpretation were to be amended in an interim period until the law was amended. In addition, modifying the interpretation would require a number of previous building permits to be reviewed and, where appropriate, re-examined. Many families, many individuals who have listed measures in confidence in granted permits, would be affected, and there would be unnecessary proceedings. The second case concerns the construction of a garage in an area reserved for construction purposes in the area area of the municipal plan. The case concerns exemptions from the provisions of the municipal plan, which require a regulatory plan before it can be built. The Ministry found that there was a basis for granting exemptions from the regulatory plan requirement in order to build this garage in this specific case. This applies to a small measure that does not affect important interests or consistency. Finally, I would like to make it clear that I believe that the decision in these two cases does not weaken the Civil Ombudsman. On the contrary, the cases, notification and consultation subsequently show that the role of the Ombudsman is very important. There is a need for an independent control body which may point to weaknesses in administrative decisions and which may contribute to resolving difficult legal issues.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-16.ana.ud346e2312	Seed funds are an important industrial policy tool, which is useful for many who start up with new ideas and create jobs. Seed funds are important for investment in the critical time phase of many companies, where lack of capital often stops both growth and further development. In 2008, the seed funds were also evaluated, and one found that the seed funds filled an investment room that was not covered by other public instruments and that each crown of state capital in the scheme triggered four kronor of private capital. The evaluation also showed that the funds created before 2000 had a lower return than expected, but that the new funds created in 2005–2007 were doing better, especially with the transfer of competence to portfolio companies. The National Audit Office has now re-examined the seed funds and the aim of the investigation was to assess the results of the scheme and the Food and Fisheries Ministry and Innovation Norway’s follow-up to the Storting decision and decisions. Seed funds are divided into three waves: wave 1, from 1998–2013, with one land cover and five regional seed funds wave 2, from 2006–2021, with four nationwide seed funds, and from 2006–2023, with five district seed funds; In addition, wave 3 started in 2013, but is not included in the survey this time, when the fund has recently been created. The main findings of the Riksrevision are that from 1998 to 2014, there are actually no large growing companies that have grown up from the seed phase, there is a high risk that several seed funds in wave 2 cannot repay loans and interest to the State, that Innovation Norway follows up the seed funds too little systematically, and that the Ministry has not provided sufficient support for the learning and follow-up of long-term results of the seed schemes. In wave 1, there is a huge loss for both the state and private, and the state loses 456 million. kr. In addition, out of 173 investments, no more than approx. 18 growing enterprises with 10 to 49 added. Similarly, in wave 2, there is a high risk that the government will not be able to recover loans and interest from either national or regional funds. And there are only four growth enterprises with 10-19 added of 67 investments on land-cover funds, and approx. 11 with growth in the value creation of the district funds. We note that the Riksrevision also says that there is a weak basis for learning and monitoring the long-term outcome of the seed funds, and that there is extensive reporting from Innovation Norway to the Ministry, but that the ongoing reporting lacks systematic analysis. The Riscrevision recommends that the Ministry should follow up the results of the Seed Fund better, such as: define what can be expected to be a good result for the overall objectives of seed schemes to produce new crops. In addition, provision must be made for reporting from Innovation Norway, which can better produce its current results and which can be used for learning and improvement. The Ministry must ensure that Innovation Norway facilitates a less vulnerable and more systematic follow-up of the schemes, and Innovation Norway should establish more systematic approaches to the follow-up of seed schemes. The Council of State has blackened the Riksrevision and itself says that there is room for improvement in the administration of both the State and the Innovation of Norway, and the Council of State states that concrete measures will be considered more closely. In the Committee we are quite a few in this matter, we are some with the recommendations of the National Audit Office, and we look forward to the concrete actions of the Council of State.
