It is free to establish itself in countries where corruption is prevalent, but it is zero tolerance for the same corruption. The message is that those who act in a country characterized by corruption must be aware of what their actions actually contribute to. They must deal with the risk of corruption, identify risk factors and have procedures for follow-up and regular review of them, and there must be a clear anchoring of management. In other words, these safeguards cannot be overlooked or sidelined. There is nothing – I underline, nothing – that is called a little tolerance for corruption. If it turns out impossible, you just have to stand out. There is a clear expectation of how companies operate, that as Transparency International puts it, they are part of the solution to the problem and not part of the problem. This applies to the highest extent where the country is the owner or the partner. The same rules apply where the State is a minority owner of a company that again owns parts of another company, as in our case with VimpelCom. There is no more or less tolerance for corruption in those cases, although it has to be done differently and is more demanding. Telenor has argued in this case that they have done a great job implementing their anti-corruption program in VimpelCom. I believe that. I'm sure they did. But at least we can now conclude that there has been no success. However, my point is that when there is no way of implementing or correcting the instruments underlying the fight against corruption, the impact of which is not beneficial, and if the deviations are unacceptable, they must withdraw, as the new Telenor group leader has decided. I also note, with satisfaction, that the Council of State has expressed the same view in a meeting she had a few days ago with 30 central directors of companies that are wholly or partly owned by the State. The Committee has registered this and will express its satisfaction with the political position and position of the Council of State in this area. So to the next: it's the police and prosecution authority that's investigating any violation of the law against corruption. Of course it is. However, it is at the heart of the work and responsibility of the Monitoring Committee, as defined in the Rules of Procedure of the European Parliament, to follow up on the fact that the decision of the Grand Duchy on state ownership is upheld. There is no contradiction between politics and the police here. Rather on the contrary, the majority gives additional authority to the police’s work in the manner in which the majority of the committee has understood its role. And as the rapporteur on the matter, I am pleased to be able to establish that a broad majority here in Stortingen agrees with such an understanding. As the last time we discussed the matter, the task of the Control Committee has been to identify the extent to which the Council of State has understood, overview and control of the company she is assigned to manage, and whether she is following up on her own and the majority of the population's policies in practice, and how active the department has been in its own dialogue with Telenor and Telenor's partnership in VimpelCom. As in the previous consultation, the Committee has also stressed whether the ownership management communicated in the ownership message, as reported in the Notice. St. 27 for 2013–2014 and adopted by the Stortinget, have been followed up. In the Communication, the expectation of the company's efforts to prevent corruption is reinforced by the Council of State of Malta, and the Stortinget has expressed what must be called zero tolerance for corruption. This case was actually closed from Stortinget's side. It was not the Storting’s own initiative that led to the case being raised again – until 30 October 2015. It was only when, in a letter dated 30 October 2015, the Minister of Economic Affairs informed the Committee that new information was available for the Ministry, and it appeared that this information was of a serious nature that the Committee decided on 3 November 2015 to reopen the case. It was also a unanimous committee that planned to hold a new hearing on this basis on 16 December 2015 – a unanimous committee. That was not the case, for the Committee received on 4 December 2015 a letter from the Advocate General showing that, in the opinion of the Public Prosecutor’s Office, there was a real risk that a hearing at that time might harm the investigation and a request that the Committee assess the possibility of postponing the hearing for six months. The request of the National Advocate was met all the time when the committee's responsibilities and duties are different from those of the police and, of course, the committee would not stand in the way of the investigation. In the meantime, the following also occurred: the first hearing’s hypothesis of corruption in relation to VimpelCom’s acquisition of telecommunications licences in Uzbekistan became true when VimpelCom announced on 17 February 2016 that the company had entered into an agreement with US and Dutch prosecution authorities. As part of the agreement, VimpelCom paid SEK 795 million. US dollars in fines and fines to US and Dutch authorities and accepted to be subject to independent external follow-up for at least three years. VimpelCom acknowledged in the agreement that corruption had taken place. One of the world’s biggest corruption cases was a fact, and a Norwegian company, Telenor, which has been operating all over the world for a number of years and has renewed great respect and trust, was involved – but not directly. And let me stress this with ‘not directly’, because a lot of attempts have been made to present our perception of this in a distorted way, especially in two areas. The first is that the control committee has never confused VimpelCom and Telenor, but in the last round we pointed out that Telenor, after all, is the largest minority victory, with 43 per cent voting weight at the general assembly in VimpelCom, and has nominated three board members to VimpelCom's board, and was veto right when the business in Uzbekistan took place. But we are fully aware that the Norwegian State has neither transparency nor control over VimpelCom as a company. The second clarification is that the Committee has never blamed Telenor’s employees nor, in any way, believed that they have actively contributed to corruption. The Committee has consistently opposed this. We've always thought and said that such a potential corruption scandal in a company that Telenor is a partner is burdensome to the employees. Another important reason why the audit hearing announced in December was to wait for Deloitte’s independent review of Telenor’s follow-up and management of the ownership of VimpelCom. It was a report that was announced to come in spring 2016, after the telenor board decided in October 2015 to commit Deloitte to a review of Telenor's follow-up and management of the ownership of VimpelCom. On 29 April 2016, Deloitte submitted the final report for the review of Telenor’s management of ownership in VimpelCom. The conclusion was that Deloitte had not found that Telenor's employees had been involved in corruption or other crimes, but discovered weaknesses internally in Telenor, as it was hot. In addition, a central part of the report referred to Telenor’s handling of a serious anxiety message in 2011 from one of Telenor’s employees, which was borrowed by VimpelCom. He communicated his concerns about possible corruption to the leaders of Telenor and Telenor's nominated board members of VimpelCom's board. And here I would like to – clearly and loudly from the spokesperson of Stortingen – to thank the alert especially for the efforts he has made. However, the concern did not go further to Telenor’s then group leader until March 2014 and to Telenor’s board in December 2014. This information was shared with the Ministry of Industry and Fisheries in October 2015. I repeat this once again, quite deliberately: it went to the board in December 2014, but did not come to the knowledge of the Ministry of Food and Fisheries until October 2015 – ten months after the telenor board was informed about the matter, ten months! And that was the reason why the Minister of Industry made a request to the magnificence and the decision of the Control Committee to reopen the case and to hold a hearing. Once these two reasons for the postponement have been explained, I would like to stress once again that the decision to hold the hearing has always been firm, even though the hearing was not held until 27 May in ours. The new information provided by the Minister of Industry meant that Storting’s treatment had taken place on the wrong premises. The Minister of Economic Affairs simply provided incomplete information to the Stortinget because it was this information she was with. On this basis, the Committee considered that a new comprehensive audit hearing was necessary. It is in itself right that the Storting should no longer reveal what the Council of State knew. She had informed us about this in her letter of 30 October. But the Committee felt it was necessary to hold a hearing on what the Council of State should be informed about, and what information should be transferred to it, and hence to the Stortinge. Incorrect information to the Stortinget is, as you know, a serious parliamentary matter. In particular, it was important for the majority to clarify the current law, adopted by the Stortinget, to prevent compliance with the decision of the Stortinge on zero tolerance for corruption, on the structures – Chinese walls, which they seem to be called – that are highlighted in the Deloitte report to have effectively prevented information from floating between VimpelCom and Telenor, and whether it was the laws that designed watertight information bulkheads between Telenor and Deleite VimpelCom. Although the hearing found that there is a somewhat different view of how the law in force is to be understood, or perhaps it was rather about how it was communicated to the committee, no matter what Professor Bråthen's message went in the direction of what he himself said: ‘You should be aware that, as a management tool, ownership of listed companies is a very sluggish and poor management tool. So the answer is that this is at least difficult." And the difficulty was in following up the zero tolerance for corruption. It would have been very bad news for all of us if it had been the conclusion. In that case, the council had to respond to how the government could go to the Storting and have passed laws that would put an effective stop to preventing corruption. Or in another way: how adopted laws prevent the wish or decision of the majority of population to zero tolerance for corruption. However, the Committee was confirmed that there was another way of looking at this. Lawyer Knudsen, who was also asked to come as an expert on company law, had, as the committee thought, a slightly different view. In his introduction it was formulated like this, and I quote parts of it: ‘From this point of view, it is quite certain that, apart from the general meeting, the ministry as a shareholder does not have a legal requirement to obtain information from Telenor. If you have a legal claim that can be enforced by means of the legal order, you must do so through the general meeting. On the other hand, there is an equally certain starting point that there is no general prohibition in the Share Act that Telenor voluntarily provides the Ministry with relevant information. The Share Act does not have a general rule on professional secrecy, nor does it generally prohibit the sharing of information between the company and the ministry. And as we are going to come back to, I believe that to some extent it must also be expected that the company will provide information that is of importance to the state that owns, especially when this has the background of a consultation in the Stortinget, and for which the Stortinget is interested." Further in the round of questions, he said: ‘It is natural that the parties in this context address issues of particular concern to shareholders, such as issues of corruption with regard to the country of ownership. These are issues that have been flagged by the state through the ownership message that you are particularly concerned about. In my view, the Ministry should then also be able to expect information on possible corruption to be provided by its own initiative, as the case may be.’ The majority of the Committee considered that the lawyer’s views and answers in the hearing were elucidative. The Committee considers it reassuring to know that laws have not been passed which preclude the follow-up to the decision of the Grand Duchy on zero tolerance for corruption. We state that the Council of State could and should have received information about the warning of corruption threats in VimpelCom, which was first held by the Director of Telenor, and which was then known to the head of the group and the board of directors. As you know, the information did not reach the Council of State before the Stortinge hearing in January 2015, nor before the plenary session of the Stortinge, in June 2015, and thus misled the Council of State to fully conclude on the management of Telenor’s reality description. Only in October 2015, after Telenor had a new head of group, the Council of State was informed that Telenor had knowledge that the Council of State could gain at a much earlier time. We believe – and I believe so – that the Council of State, in a exemplary way, passed on the new information without delay to the Committee. One consequence of the new information being revealed was that the director of Telenor went off as a result of a lack of trust from the Council of State. Deloitt underlined in the hearing that what is in their report is that the Statement of Concern, which emerged in 2011, «had come to Telenor's management and to the Board's knowledge at an earlier date. [[...]] the severity of the notification of concern should have been taken into account in the assessment of the information to be provided to the owner.’;» The Committee stresses that the criticism in the Deloittes report against Telenor’s management is much stronger than it has been tried to make as by those it primarily concerns: former group head and former head of telenor’s board. The Committee takes note of the report by Mr Deloitte. The report speaks for itself about what has actually happened in the case. We record that the new management of Telenor, as well as the head of the group, has stated that they believe this was a very serious and difficult matter for Telenor. We are pleased that they add that it has also provided an important lesson, and that they have already started to implement the improvement points set out in the Deloitte report. It is an independent and important point for the Control Committee that matters should not be repeated. And I'm adding: An important message from Stortinget in this case is that zero tolerance for corruption is serious. The Committee's extensive consideration of this matter sends a message to Norwegian industry in general and to state-owned companies in particular: corruption is unacceptable, and the Stortinget will follow up the Council's efforts to maintain zero tolerance. Finally, we are very pleased that the State Council of Malta has underlined the value of Storting's work on the matter. She also underlined the important difference from the previous treatment here in the Stortinget to this, namely that VimpelCom has admitted to having committed corruption. And we find that the State Council of Malta, through its ownership dialogue, did not have the opportunity to discover whether Telenor followed up the reinforced effort against corruption, which is determined by the ownership message and the Stortinge's decision on zero tolerance for corruption. However, what we do not get by, and which is serious enough in view of the trust-based ownership we have in Norway, is that those states councils should have confidence, the Telenor government, did not prove to be worthy of this trust. The current Chairman and the then Group Head of Telenor led the Council of State, the last time the matter was discussed in the Control Committee, by not informing her of information about the concerns they both knew about, before she met in the committee's hearing. Because it was appropriate for the then management to hold the information back, they sent the government council to the Storting with incorrect information. It says a lot about respect for the largest owner. I would also like to say that their behaviour towards Stortinget is under any criticism. From the former group leader and former director of Telenor, special attention was paid to the fact that they would be open and truthful, but at the same time they spoke wrongly and incompletely to the Storing. On the one hand, they considered that the management of the notification of concerns was not important because, according to the lawyer company Hjort, it was not contrary to Norwegian law and, on the other hand, that the information should not be disclosed for the purpose of the investigation. After VimpelCom has now acknowledged corruption and paid a record fine, it places these attitudes in a very special light. In the end, the Council of State brought to Stortinget what the Council of State had knowledge of. In this way, she gave the Stortinget wrong information, because she had confidence that the rules of good ownership and the laws to which a company that Telenor had to relate had been followed. We do not blame the Council of State, but believe that this case has revealed that the State that owns must assess whether trust is sufficient or whether other instruments also need to be assessed. A majority also believe that the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Fisheries, when the case was brought to the Department in October 2015, could have chosen to examine Telenor rather than leave Telenor to take that decision. We are well told that today the Stortinget is dealing with the latest report from the Control and Constitutional Committee on Telenor and this matter. The issue is factually limited – or should I perhaps clarify: should be limited in substance – to parliamentary control of the State's stake in Telenor by the Council of State and the Ministry. In addition to the operational decision that we attach this considerable report to the protocol, this will also give rise to a number of points of view. The overall position of the Committee is that the Committee is satisfied with the way in which the State Council and the Ministry manage State shareholdings in Telenor. For my part, I feel the need – not least after hearing the previous posts – to emphasise that we are satisfied that Telenor, our Norwegian Telenor, cannot be charged for corruption actions carried out by VimpelCom in Uzbekistan. Actually, the matter could have been supported by a unanimous committee opinion, something similar to that held in the introductory part. But I think the review we got needs some additional perspectives. I choose to take a slightly general perspective. Few of them could have imagined that the state company Televerket, which managed a telephone queue around 1980, would have done so. 100 000 according to best planning economic principles, would form the basis for today's Telenor, which is one of the most successful technology companies in our global corner. The assumption was that the state reorganised ownership of what became a listed company, with a significant element of private co-investors, with the arm's length principle from politicians to those who are working in the market and engaged in technology development, and with internationalisation, professional governance and professional management. I have sometimes seen that someone is calling for the national strategists of the kind we had when the Hydro adventure was launched at the beginning of the 20th century. I believe that we have national strategists, and that there were just people like Tormod Hermansen and Jon Fredrik Baksaas who, in the technological development of the last two decades, have stood for a national strategy. In our company, then, there were directors who are in other contexts very norms, such as Eivind Reten, Vidar Rygh, Harald Norvik, and I would also mention Svein Aaser, since he was suspended in another context, who has contributed to the great value creation that has also made the state's shareholding much more valuable and underpinned employment and value creation in Norway in a completely different way than if one had continued as a state company completely under ministerial control and control. I do not overlook the fact that it is still the way it is managed, which may also have influenced the view of how to act as owner of a public limited-liability company. Thus, the transition from a state-owned company governed by direct departmental governance, almost in line with a directorate, to a listed company of international dimensions has contributed to an enormous value creation to the joy of the community. This does not mean that the public governance of the company is weakened. But it's going to be different. Political detail management is indicated, but the company is subject to laws and regulations that are the same for all – laws and regulations adopted in this Chamber. This means that the State that also owns is subject to its own law, whether it is the public stock exchange law, the securities trading law or the stock exchange regulation – and of course what became central: criminal provisions against corruption in Norway and not least internationally. That's how it has to be in a state of law. The way in which the State manages its ownership is described in several large-scale communications, and to date there has been broad agreement on the principles – not least because ownership is divided into groups, according to the purposes and purposes of the companies, and what the State wants with its ownership of them. The ownership should be of value to all, but it must be done in a social context that takes care of the environment and people and, of course, respects the international and national legal order. As a listed company, ownership must be exercised through Telenor’s own corporate bodies, i.e. the general meeting and, on a daily basis, the Management Board. This ownership must be exercised in compliance with exchange rules — the fact that, at the same time as all others, the public has requirements for information which may affect their demand for shares — and for joint owners, i.e. other private shareholders in the company. As part of the wide internationalisation, Telenor has had a minority position in VimpelCom, which has not given control of this company. On the other hand, VimpelCom is listed in New York under American law. And it's the U.S. authorities that have demonstrated zero tolerance against corruption, and that, together with the prosecution authorities of other countries, have led the investigation into the corruption case in Uzbekistan, where VimpelCom paid a lot of money for a 4G license to the president's nearest circuit. The corruption case has been closed. VimpelCom has adopted a record fine of 6 billion. Norwegian kroner against releasing evidence and contributing to the clarification of the matter. They have also submitted to a stricter control regime for a three-year period. International police cooperation and widespread criminal prosecution ended with a historically high corporate penalty — well deserved. The criminal prosecution of VimpelCom should delight followers of international economic relations and remind skeptics that the alternative to the global exchange of services, goods and capital is not isolation, not customs walls and not protectionism, but an ever-increasing expansion of the international legal order, with effective law enforcement. That's what we got. So that's the transition from our dearest Telenor to the international arena. It is a reminder to choose your colleagues and partners with care. Telenor had in a transition status as a witness to the matter, but is now out of business. That's what I think Telenor's many employees had deserved to hear from this pulpit. Telenor has appointed members of the board for his minority position in VimpelCom, but Telenor is not accused of anything, nor is he accused of any official authority on the occasion of the case. The company has allowed an examination of its own conduct throughout the course, a report that was completed by the law firm Deloitte. In this respect, there is proper criticism of individuals based on Telenor, but who worked in VimpelCom, and they also reap criticism for having calmed down that the corruption charges in Uzbekistan should have been examined in greater detail, and also that they did not disclose information at an earlier stage. The evaluation of individuals may be called into question, but during the hearing the Norwegian Director of VimpelCom was given a review of a number of measures, including by external agencies in the United States, which responded to the rumors but thus discontinued the investigations. These studies are also claimed to be derived from falsified evidence from adulterous officials in VimpelCom. There is still research on individuals that can be held responsible for what has happened in VimpelCom, but we are definitely out of what you can do about Telenor. I do not think the rapporteur's posts were well enough signposted between individuals based on Telenor and what a whole company is involved in. It has been arduous at all to keep the party relations in order, but it is also part of the work of examining from a parliamentary point of view, of keeping track of both the regulations and the rules in force, and not least of the conditions of the parties. In any case – the remaining uncertainty that is of public interest in the case is whether there are persons associated with Norway who, as a position in VimpelCom, may be among the officials to whom the company will address the responsibility, and that remains to be seen. Under Norwegian law, bribery and corruption may be subject to criminal prosecution for Norwegian citizens even if the action has been carried out abroad. These cases will therefore be concluded by the prosecution authority and by the courts. I again find reason to stress that Telenor does not have any unfinished business with the police authorities, but at a certain stage of the matter in a transition has been required to have a test report. No reproach is directed to Telenor as a company. ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-16.ana.ud346e2764	The issue we are discussing today is primarily what ownership management it has been on the part of the State Council in Telenor's handling of the VimpelCom case in view of the principles of ownership notification. US dollars, it's important to clarify how the Council of State has followed up the requirement of zero tolerance for corruption against Telenor. The Committee has, on two occasions, unanimously decided to open proceedings and to carry out verification hearings. Both the aim has been to examine how the Council of State has followed up the adopted ownership policy of the Grand Duchy in practice. The Committee has been aware that the role of investigative body has been delegated to police and prosecution authorities. Our task has been to assess the ownership management carried out by the Council of State in the context of the information available at all times. The Council of State has also characterised the matter as serious and no longer trusted the former Chairman of the Board of Directors. Then it can be said, and I hear some say, that when the management of Telenor acknowledges critical relationships, there is an internal relationship in Telenor and between Telenor and the company's owners, and that the role of the state is primarily about ownership managed by the Ministry of State and through the established ownership systems. It should therefore be the Committee that is not competent. It is right that ownership should be managed through the Ministry and the structures established, which the Storting has decided to apply to the exercise of State ownership. It is true that this is the essence, but then parliamentary control also comes into the picture. We cannot ignore that in this matter, in our opinion. As the elected body of any government emerges from, it is precisely the task of the Storting to control the exercise of ownership that takes place and that is decided by the Storting. We're going to monitor how the Storting's decision on zero tolerance for corruption is followed up. Then I said earlier and said it again: The Storting controls the government and, in this case, the departmental council responsible. When we are going to prepare a case for the Storting related to this, we have to, in our opinion, go below the surface. Then the information from Telenor is relevant for informing the matter. We need to deal with the information that emerges, which forms the basis for the Committee's assessment of whether the government and the State Council have followed up the Storting's decision. Then the Telenor leadership's recognition is not just a matter for Telenor and the owners. The recognition and the information leading up to recognition are also relevant and important for the Committee and for the Storting. No, neither the Committee nor the Storting shall exercise operational ownership. We don't think we do that either. On the other hand, the Committee is to do its job, namely to determine whether the Council of State has done what it can to ensure that the decision on zero tolerance for corruption of the Storting has been followed up. That's the committee's job, and that's what we've done in this case. Therefore, in the view of the Christian People's Party, consultation number two was important with a view to obtaining relevant views on the basis of the critical Deloitte report presented. It was also important to clarify the background to the state council's view of the matter and the understanding of her handling. The Deloitte report provides a basis for a case that has been largely endorsed by everyone. Although relevant aspects of the case, such as: how the Telenor-nominated members of the board of VimpelCom handled the corruption suspicions, have not been informed, nevertheless, on the basis of the Committee's knowledge of the matter, there is every reason to believe that the report points to the very central aspects of what has been happening and why things have happened as they have done. One of the most important aspects of the criticism raised in the Deloitte report was that it was not put up for an internal construction in the Telenor group that could address the warnings of corruption suspicion in a more comprehensive way, while at the same time preserving the requirement of confidentiality. The answers given in the hearing of both invited experts on corporate light and the current director and group manager were that such a requested design as the Deloitte report would have been fully feasible. As Chairman of the Management Board, Værsted concluded during the hearing: ‘And it’s clear that with the focus that it has now been on this matter, I don’t think there are any in Telenor who are in doubt that there are doubts and concerns that are directed towards criminal acts such as corruption, so it should be lifted up in the system.’ It was reassuring to hear. Together with the Deloitte report, it was the different descriptions of what was known in terms of corruption, which was one of the main reasons why the committee found it appropriate to reopen the case again in the autumn of 2015. There can be no doubt that the former group manager and former director did not communicate what they knew at the time of the first consultation in January 2015. Both later claimed that the information on suspicion of corruption was not important because, according to the law firm Hjort, they were not handled in a way that was contrary to Norwegian law. Furthermore, when this information was not provided to the Council of State for the same reason, there is reason to conclude that both the Council of State and the Stortninget were taken away from the light. It is therefore important to stress from the Christian People's Party that we fully support the reaction of the Council of State to the then Governor when she became aware of this information. We experience that she has acted in an exemplary way and has taken the Storting's claim to zero tolerance for corruption in the deepest seriousness. And we'll notice that, and we'll appreciate that. Then this is not the only case where Norwegian state-owned companies have either been involved in suspicion of corruption or have been convicted of corruption. In view of recent years' cases of this nature, there is reason to raise the issue of the degree to which the Norwegian management system works well enough. Is the ownership dialogue close enough between the state that owns and the board that is responsible for the company's operations, and especially when a company chooses to start operations in countries where the corruption factor is particularly high? And is it that the pursuit of profit and profit is easily hindered by the care that one must have in all business activities when an operator ashore where corruption is common? For the Norwegian companies, which have been involved in corruption, the problem does not seem to be a lack of anti-corruption programmes. What's been missing is non-compliance. In some of these cases, it may seem that there has simply been insufficient preparation for the establishment of land-based activities with a high degree of corruption culture. Lack of knowledge of the market, lack of knowledge of the country to be established and the business partners to be dealt with seem, according to Transparency International, to be some of the most important conditions that create the basis for getting involved in a culture of corruption. There is reason to ask to what extent state companies have been adequately prepared for these challenges, and to what extent strategies can be put in place to meet these challenges. In the next round, there is reason to ask questions about the country’s role as owner. The way in which awareness is generated among companies is that not only corruption programmes are necessary in these areas, but strategies for dealing with such situations. An important question to ask the Council of State today is whether she feels secure that she has sufficient and proper tools to ensure that corporate governance takes responsibility for the development of these kinds of strategies in the most serious way. On the part of the Christian People's Party, we also want a clear state owner with a clear, forward-looking attitude towards the governments of the companies in terms of demands for zero corruption. We want the state that owns to be clear on what expectations you have of the companies in this field. This does not mean that intelligence activities are to be carried out by the State, but that a clear requirement is made and not least has tools that enable expectations to be met in practice. For the Christian People's Party, as for the majority of the Committee, it was important to carry out these hearings of control that could tell us something about the government's ownership management in the light of the demands of the Storting to zero tolerance for corruption. These have given us answers that make us more sure that Telenor as a company has learned some of the criticisms it has received. Not least, we are behind a state council that has clearly shown that she is taking the Storting's will in this area in the most serious way, and has shown some action in a situation that required a response. I would otherwise like to agree with the rapporteur's thorough and thorough consideration. I would also like to pay tribute to the rapporteur and the chairman of the committee for the way he has been clear and upright in the debate both inside and outside the walls of this house.

Norwegian Krone. Not having access to this agreement is a major weakness for the Committee's consideration and setting up. There is a great deal of political consensus in the committee when dealing with this matter, but the right, as stated by the representative Tetzschner, strongly disagrees. On behalf of the Centre Party, I agree with the opinion of the Committee Chairman Kolberg on the Right's position on the matter. The right-hand long comment cannot be understood as different from the unwillingness to clarify the matter, and therefore also the consequence of the case: that lessons are learned from it, so that ownership can be changed by the department, and so that the will of the Storting, zero tolerance for corruption, gets a much more precise and, for the companies, more demanding content. Large words about ethics and zero tolerance can no longer be presented in an equally naive way to the Stortinget after this treatment. I want to go a little further into the company's responsibility to provide the Council with the right information. According to the opinion of the Centre Party, the general rule on the confidentiality of directors is that when one goes out with information to other than the company’s management, it must be a result of the decision of the Board of Directors – i.e. it is the Board of Directors that decides what is said to other than the management of the company. However, the degree of professional secrecy of each member of the board must be balanced against the considerations of substance. It is reasonably clear that the professional secrecy of board members cannot be used as a reason to hide criminal relations. In case of suspicion of corruption that individual members of the board are given access to, in my opinion, a Norwegian member of the board of VimpelCom has a maritime responsibility to report their suspicion to the Executive Director and the Board of Directors of Telenor, and they are then responsible for notifying this to the State as a partner in Telenor. For information only: Members of the Board of Directors of Telenor of VimpelCom Ltd. was in 2011 Jon Fredrik Baksaas, Kjell Morten Johnsen and Ole Bjørn Sylstad. Following my assessment of the obligation of professional secrecy, Telenor’s then CEO, Jon Fredrik Baksaas, who was a member of the board of directors of VimpelCom in 2009, 2010, in 2012, in 2012 and in 2013, i.e. for five years, and former director of Telenor, should have raised with the Council of State suspicions of corruption in the many meetings that have been held over the years. If these two leaders of Telenor did not take it up, they had to be sure that other people informed the State Council of the suspicions. Such an approach is absolutely necessary when you know the background for the establishment of VimpelCom. The company was established over time with the Norwegian and Russian Heads of State Putin and Bondevik as Bisitters. We all remember very well the picture from the government's representative residence on November 12, 2002, where Putin and Bondevik stood behind, and the two group leaders signed, and one of them was Baksaas. It was openly recognised that the different business cultures created major problems in the management of the company, and this should clearly indicate that the state council representing the Norwegian government had a special responsibility to monitor the development of the company. Special vigilance had to be established to prevent corruption, which was a major problem for all recognized countries to obtain government-approved licences in countries such as the United Kingdom. Uzbekistan – a country with a very bad reputation in this area. VG had a notice on 7 October 2009 on the very establishment of VimpelCom Ltd., which found that it was a company that had its head office in the Netherlands, that the parent company was to be registered on the tax haven of Bermuda, that the scheme was chosen for legal and tax reasons, according to Telenor, and that it was to be quoted in the United States. The then State Council Brustad said in her press release about the matter that she made a reservation that she had not had the opportunity to go into all the business aspects of the agreement. It was then for me a strong statement. In retrospect, we see that one has to go into matters more thoroughly, and here the councils have a considerable responsibility. Telenor ordered the investigation report from Deloitte. Telenor thus also defined the mandate for this investigation. In my opinion, it should be the department that ordered the review report. As part of the Ministry's mandate, the review should also have included the administration and the board of directors in Telenor's position on the matter over time. If the Ministry had not received notice of this at an extraordinary general meeting in Telenor, they would have had to have requested a share law review under the Share Act, which they have occasion to do. In my opinion, there is a great weakness in the Committee's consideration of the matter that we have not received such an investigation, which would also inform the matter at the level of responsibility in addition to VimpelCom. A central concept from the Stortinget towards the government in the management of state-owned companies is the concept of ‘zero tolerance for corruption’. What is there is the matter is that, as far as I have found, there is only one place it is mentioned in a committee note, and it is in the committee note to the Inst. S. No 163 for 2006–2007, which was issued on 29 March 2007 in connection with the notification of St. Announcement. No 13 for 2006-2007. It's called on s. 18: ‘The Committee expects that all companies with State ownership will practice zero tolerance against corruption and have good systems in place to control it.’ There are other wordings that have been followed up in the Council's letter of reply to the Committee of our letter of 17 February 2015. The Committee asked: ‘The Committee would like information on the warning systems requested by the Ministry of State and jointly owned companies. Including an assessment of Telenor’s handling of this special alert matter.’» In the letters of the Council of State, it states: «As shown in the ownership message, Notify. St. 27, which was dealt with by the Stortinget on 5 February, expects the government to have good warning systems in its own business. [[2013-2014]] The companies themselves are responsible for this. The management of these companies has been the subject of the ownership dialogue with the companies, inter alia, during the annual meetings on the social responsibility of the companies. In addition, the Ministry and companies, such as employers, are subject to the rules on alerts laid down in the Working Environment Act.» So the Ministry has considered it this way, according to Storting's comments, that it is the companies that are responsible. The major weakness of the concept of ‘zero tolerance for corruption’ is that the content is not defined. It is far from satisfactory only to terpe upon this heading without giving it any more details as to how a company's management should behave in order to avoid corruption. In my opinion, the government has to give the concept content and get to the Storting with it in an appropriate way, so that the level of awareness of what needs to be done to prevent corruption is raised considerably. In my opinion, the best proof of zero tolerance must be whether companies can demonstrate a sound zero tolerance practice. It has done so if, when faced with a concrete and difficult choice, it has made a balance between interesting business opportunities with good performance on the one hand, and a high risk of corruption if the business project is carried out, so-called compliance dilemma on the other hand. The company must document, if there is any outcome, that the business here came out of the possibility of high risk of corruption. This will be a good indisium on compliance with its own control regime. In my opinion, this kind of decision must be taken by top management, which will provide good evidence that management is at the forefront of anti-corruption work. The further prerequisite for the control system is that management instructs downwards in the company on the risk of corruption, management procedures, identification of specific risk elements, their concrete follow-up and regular tightening and refresher of procedures. In my opinion, three points need to be satisfied in order to be able to talk about zero tolerance for corruption: the company must 1) document previous management of corruption barriers; 2) document how they have achieved their business objectives without corruption in countries known for extensive corruption; and 3) document a zero tolerance training system and how it has been implemented in its own top management and governance – i.e. maritime. I would like to point out that in any such case, where there have been maladministration, the best lesson for the organisation is that those who have had overall responsibility step back. It's diplomatically said, step back. In my opinion, it is not to take responsibility when a person is sitting in his or her position while he or she says that the situation is now corrected for the future. Simple organisational psychology shows that resignation from the position works best throughout the organisation. In my opinion, I would like to point out that the size of the corporate fine is approx. 6 B.C.E. kr, and that the content of the Deloitte report is so alarming and the risk of new corruption surprises is so great that the Ministry has to take the initiative to re-examine — this is in order to get a complete review of all Telenor’s activities abroad, especially in Eastern Europe, Thailand, India and Myanmar, in order to monitor the practice of zero tolerance for corruption. The National Audit Office – and I would like to stress this – must be given the professional responsibility for this work, and that it is carried out in a thorough manner on the basis of the decisions and attitudes of the Norwegian Storing against corruption. I will also give tribute to the President-in-Office for thorough work, and for having managed to deal with this matter through the storm in which it has stood. It serves the chairman of the committee to the credit.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-16.ana.ud346e3074	Corruption poses a threat to the rule of law, democracy, human rights and social justice. In addition, corruption prevents economic development and it distorts competition. Therefore, corruption is illegal, and so the state that owns has clear expectations of the company's work and action against corruption, which we follow up. As I stated in the verification hearing in the case on 27 May, it is important to point out that there are no telenors or employees in Telenor who are charged with corruption. However, it is serious that corruption is revealed in VimpelCom, which is a company in which Telenor has had a significant ownership exposure over time. The matter has had a major impact on Telenor, and it has raised fundamental questions about the role of the Department and the framework of state ownership policy. The expectations associated with the company's efforts to prevent corruption in its operations are an important part of government ownership policy. The expectations in this area were reinforced in the ownership communication presented in June 2014 and adopted by the Stortinget last year. So it's that no one can guarantee that corruption or any other form of crime will never happen, but we have to do what we can to prevent it. My responsibility in this matter has been to manage the state's ownership of Telenor within the framework set by the Stortinget for state ownership policy. There are frameworks on which there has been cross-political agreement and which have been stuck for a long time. I have explained for several rounds the ministry's handling of this matter. As I have stated earlier, I believe that the department has followed up the matter in a thorough manner and with the seriousness of the matter. In my time, the VimpelCom case and corruption have been the subject of the ownership dialogue with Telenor in over 30 meetings, as well as written presentations. I am also pleased that a majority of the committee does not appear to object to the ministry's handling. This matter was fundamentally changed when I received new and significant information about the role of Telenor in October 2015. I then received information that concerns related to VimpelCom and corruption had been addressed at a high level in Telenor's own organisation back in 2011. This is information that had been made known to the Chairperson at an earlier date, and before the hearing in January 2015 and the deliberation in June of the same year. It is my opinion that Telenor's then director should have communicated to the department that he put more and more material information in the matter at an earlier time. This was also the background that I could no longer trust him as the director of Telenor. I also record that Deloitte's review provides support for the factual basis related to the assessments I then made. This has been a major issue that has raised many issues of a different nature, including those related to state ownership policy. I also record that the matter has started a fundamental debate and disagreement in the committee's position on this matter. This is linked to the actual implications of the established framework for State ownership, both for the State as owner and also for the role of the Control and Constitutional Committee. This is an important debate, and there is a discussion that lies at Stortinget to take. As the Council of State, of course, it is not up to me to enter into such a debate. However, let me say in general: the fact that there has been a cross-political agreement over time on the basic framework of state ownership, and that these have been clearly communicated and have been fixed over time, has been crucial for the success of Norwegian state ownership. It has contributed to the good development of companies and employees, it has also contributed to the good value creation of owners, including for the State, and it has contributed to the trust in the Norwegian capital market. Let me also briefly comment on another aspect of the position, and it is linked to the fact that a majority of the Committee points out that this case has revealed that the State that owns the need to assess whether trust is an adequate instrument in the follow-up to state ownership or whether other instruments need to be assessed. Trust is by no means sufficient. The State exercises its ownership by, inter alia, to take a formal position on matters at the general meeting and to assess the follow-up to the country's expectations based on the company's reporting to the market. But trust is also absolutely necessary. From my perspective as the Council of State and as responsible for the exercise of a significant part of state ownership, I would like to stress that we rely on a fundamental trust relationship between a large owner and a company's board of directors to perform both the role of owner and as the board of directors in a good way. We also rely on such a trust-based relationship to have a good ownership dialogue. That's the way it is, and that's the way it has to be. That is also why there are serious consequences when this trust is lost, as happened in this case. Finally, let me show that this has been a comprehensive and demanding matter, which has also had significant consequences for Telenor. It is important that the company learns from this difficult case. Let me also repeat what I said in the verification hearing: it is important that we all now contribute to the creation of peace around the company, primarily in the interests of the employees, but also so that the company's board of directors and management can focus in the future on re-establishing trust in the company and taking action to ensure that the company will not deal with these kinds of issues in the future.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-16.ana.ud346e3369	I am now delighted to present a unanimous opinion from the Supervisory and Constitutional Committee on the National Audit Office's notification of the activities in 2015. The National Audit Office has provided a full report, which well describes the work and activities of the National Audit Office in 2015. Both this and previous messages show that the work of the National Audit Office is important for the Storting to carry out its control of government and administration in a good way. It follows from the recommendation that the Supervisory and Constitutional Committee is also satisfied this year with the annual financial audit covering 15 departments, the Prime Minister’s Office and 227 underlying State activities for the financial year 2014. Furthermore, the Committee has noted that the use of resources in corporate control has increased in line with the intentions of the strategic plan and new organisation. The control of the management of state companies for 2014 included 124 companies and activities broken down into wholly and jointly owned companies, health enterprises, state enterprises, special-law companies and student partnerships. I will again underline the importance of company control. When the government organises important parts of its activity in companies, it is important that company control is good. There must be real control in order to also contribute to the learning and guidance of management in the future. The National Audit Board has sent 10 management reports in Document 3 series to Stortinget in 2015. These management reports have put important issues on the agenda. These reports are essential for the Storting to receive relevant information on the implementation and impact of public action. The recommendations of the National Audit Office mainly concern the need to develop more overall and knowledge-based governance, the need for ministries and businesses to make greater use of the space for action provided by given instruments, and the need to clarify responsibilities and strengthen coordination measures. In this respect, I would like to point out that the Committee on Control and Constitutional Affairs is generally inclined to agree with the conclusions and recommendations of the management reports. That means they're good. There's a lot of evidence that there's a good interaction between the National Audit Office and the administration. The national audit carries out user surveys for the different types of audits each year. This year, too, the Committee noted that user surveys generally produce good results. This applies to all types of audits. It tells us that the Riksrevision is an organisation that has a great deal of respect for the administration, and that the basics are a good relationship between the Riksrevision and the administration, although sometimes there are also disagreements. When dealing with this annual report, it is important to remember that we generally have good governance in Norway. But as I have pointed out from this pulpit earlier, parts of the administration have problems following up what is being pointed out by the Riksrevision. It is a problem, and it is important that the administration takes on the lessons of the Riksaudition’s extensive audit activities. The National Audit is celebrating its 200th anniversary this year. In this anniversary year, we can remember that the Riksrevision is one of the state institutions that has the longest roots in Norway – formally and effectively. It is remarkable that, from the very beginning, our parliamentary ancestors have given so much attention to the control of the government of the Grand Duchy. Parliamentary control is of great importance in our democracy. The national audit is and will continue to be the main provider of control cases by the Storting. The need for large-scale control is large and complex. It also imposes great demands on the National Audit Office in the future. Finally, I would like to say a little bit about the National Audit Office's international work and activities. Norway has a strong national audit — also internationally. The Committee welcomes the participation of the National Audit Office in international audit missions and assists in the development of audits in other countries. The Committee agrees with the annual report that independent and effective national audits are a prerequisite for ensuring proper control of the public sector and the fight against corruption. The Committee has also noted that the Riksrevision itself considers that international work facilitates the strengthening of the Riksrevision’s knowledge in areas that are important for the Riksrevision. That's good. Both in this message and in previous messages, the Stortinget clearly shows that the Riksrevision’s work is the basis for the Stortinget to carry out the task of controlling government and administration in a very good way – and in the way that both the Constitution and other legislation actually provide the basis. The Committee agrees with the content of the National Audit Office's communication, and I repeat that it is a unanimous committee that presents this position to the Stortinget.

Nursing in the sense that we know it today was born through the work of Florence Nightingale. She was a pioneer in modern nursing and established the first secular nursing school in the world, in London. She was an innovation and used tools as statistical data presented in tables and charts, and used them as powerful arguments for medical reform. Such statistics are some of the reasons why we are debating here today. Today’s age tends to have complex and multiple diseases. Body and soul hang together and sometimes challenge scientific medicine. The background to this proposal is that the Ministry of Culture of Prop. 1 S for 2015–2016 recommended opening a private archive storage solution through the rental of privately owned premises. Before the government enters into any lease with a private operator for the storage of this archive-worthy material, it is important, in the opinion of the proposers, that a thorough assessment be made of the consequences of giving this responsibility to a private limited liability company. It's been about ten years since NOU 2006:5, Norwegian health records – the last stop to the patient records, was handed over to the Ministry of Health and Welfare. The NOU states that the main task of the Norwegian health archive — the depository function itself — will be to take care of ‘older, conservation-worthy patient archives’ from the specialist health service and to manage them for later use for research and other documentation purposes. The NOU also recommends that the Norwegian health archive be established by the state and operated under state control on the assumption. The investigation strongly recommends that the Norwegian health archive be located together in one geographical location and operated by the State. ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-16.ana.ud346e3830	Initially, I would like to agree with the proposers that managing national health information in a reliable and safe manner is a very important societal task. As the members of the Committee from the Central Party, the Right and the Advance Party pointed out in the vote, the government has briefed the Stortinget on the state of play of the government's work on the construction of the Norwegian health archive in the context of the revised national budget. The government has informed us that we will return to Stortinget on this matter by the end of 2016 when the investigations have been carried out and the decision-making basis is considered sufficient. The review of the budget today and the position of the Financial Committee will be part of the further work. I am therefore pleased to see that the Financial Committee's majority in the Inner East. 400 S for 2015–2016 are committed to finding a good solution for the health archive. I will allow myself to comment on some of the comments in the position of the Health and Care Committee. The representatives of the Workers' Party and the Centre believe that the 2006 public investigation recommends that Norwegian health archives be collected in one place. I believe that it can be discussed whether the sample's recommendation on location was so unambiguous. In any case, the NOU has created a completely different digital reality for the work in Norwegian health archives than is the case today – a lot has happened on the technology front in the last ten years. In the 2006 study, the digitisation of journals to be used in health research was considered a possible ‘extended service offer on a mission basis’. What was then a hopeful idea of what could become possible is today reality. Norwegian health archives shall be built in accordance with the provisions of the Health Archives Regulation, as laid down in March of this year. The Health Archives Regulation has taken account of technological developments since the NOU came ten years ago. Norwegian health archives will receive about 100,000 shelving metres of paper-based archives from specialist health services in the next decades. In addition, existing and future digitally generated material is added. Of the 100,000 shelf meters, everything shall be digitised as far as possible. Norwegian health archive will be a complete digital archive depot. Only approx. 12,000 of these shelving meters, mainly older patient archives, shall remain in physical form after digitisation. The remainder shall be digitised and discarded. Thus, there will be no need for large areas for the storage of physical archive material. The organisation of health research will take place digitally. The complete digital depot for Norwegian health archives will be established at Tynset, where this work will take place. I note the Committee's recommendation to look at the challenges that may arise in the context of cooperation with private actors. Any decision to re-examine this is, in my view, unnecessary. But the government will of course follow up the decisions of Stortinget about Norwegian health records. In this context, it will be important for the realisation of Norwegian health archives that we, as policy makers, take into account both technological development and our political responsibility for the best possible use of Community money. It involves a critical look at the ‘truths of yesterday’ and avoiding the fact that decisions on any new investigation actually involve the outsourcing of Norwegian health records. I believe – as the majority of the Committee on Budgets also states in the vote on the revised national budget – that a solution for the deposit and location of paper documents should be resolved in the most cost-effective way possible. It is implicit in this that the patient information should, of course, be stored safely.
